(87)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0,1,2,3}(\tr{\widetilde\sigma_i\rho})\bra{x}V^{\dagger}U^{\dagger}\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}UV\ket{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=0,1,2,3}(\tr{\widetilde\sigma_i\rho})(\tr{U^\dag\widetilde\sigma_iU\widetilde\sigma_j })\bra{x}V^{\dagger}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}V\ket{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j,k=0,1,2,3}(\tr{\widetilde\sigma_i\rho})(\tr{U^\dag\widetilde\sigma_iU\widetilde\sigma_j})(\tr{V^\dag\widetilde\sigma_j V \widetilde\sigma_k})(\tr{\widetilde\sigma_k\ketbra{x}{x}}),

where we used Eq. (
85
) iteratively.

While now there are roughly
4^{Nd}
different paths (because we have
\propto Nd
space-time positions and 4 basis matrices), the weights of different paths can be vastly different, as contrasted with the nearly-uniform distribution of weights in the computational-basis path integral. In particular,
a path in the Pauli basis has a weight exponentially decaying with the number of non-trivial Paulis (i.e., other than the identity). This can be understood by the fact that the transition
I\to I
satisfies
|\tr{U^\dag I U I}|>|\tr{U^\dag\sigma U \sigma^\prime}|
, if any of
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
is not
I
(Clifford gates are a special case where should use
\geq
). Thus, a transition involving a non-trivial Pauli (
X,Y,Z
) will cause a decay relative to the transition involving only
I
. The path involving only
I
s corresponds to the contribution which is cancelled by
-1
in the definition of the XEB.

Modified algorithms to refute XQUATH.

For a circuit architecture with every two-qubit gates being surrounded by single-qubit Haar random gates, we first apply our analytical tools to get its corresponding diffusion-reaction model. The modified algorithm for this circuit architecture will first cut out a subsystem (which might not be the straightforward way we did in the original algorithm) of constant width in space. Then the algorithm fully simulates this subsystem in polynomial time (since it has constant width in space) and samples output string according to the marginal distribution of the output layer with the output qubits outside being sampled from the uniform distribution. In the following, we describe how to select a subsystem for a circuit architecture and then argue that the modified algorithm indeed achieves average XEB at least
e^{-O(d)}
.

First, recall from
Equation 17
and
Equation 21
that the partition function of the diffusion-reaction model
\chi_{\text{av}}+1
has input boundary
{\mathbf{u}}=(1/2\ 1/2)
and output boundary
\mathbf{v}_{\text{\tiny XEB}}=(2\ 2/3)
in the Pauli basis
\{I,\Omega\}^{l}
where
\Omega
denotes the non-trivial Pauli.
As the contribution of both the input and output boundary being all
I
s is
1
, we have that
\chi_{\text{av}}
corresponds to the contribution from boundaries containing non-Pauli’s.

Next, we focus on a boundary condition with both input and output boundary having exactly one non-trivial Pauli and argue that its contribution to the partition function is at least
e^{-O(d)}
. Before continuing, we reorganize
{\mathbf{u}}=(11)
and output boundary
\mathbf{v}_{\text{\tiny XEB}}=(11/3)
for convenience.
Notice that the transfer matrix of our diffusion reaction model provides a way to calculate the weight of each path in the Pauli basis (see subsubsection
Properties of the transfer matrix
T^{(G)}
). In particular, as the reaction rate
R
is at most
2/3
, there is always a non-zero probability to move from a configuration of exactly one non-trivial Pauli to another configuration of exactly one non-trivial Pauli. Thus, we can start with an input boundary having exactly one non-trivial Pauli and repeat the above argument to move it to an output boundary with exactly one non-trivial Pauli. Specifically, this path has weight at least
\max(1-D,D-R)^{d}/3\geq 6^{-d}/3
.

Finally, we simply select a path of exactly one non-trivial Pauli and select the subsystem containing the gates that correspond to the locations of these non-trivial Pauli’s. Then the algorithm simply fully simulates this subsystem and samples output string from the marginal distribution with the other output qubits being sampled from the uniform distribution.
Note that the partition function of the corresponding diffusion-reaction model is at least
1
plus the weight of this subsystem, which is at least
e^{-O(d)}
as discussed in the previous paragraph. Thus, we have an efficient algorithm that achieves average XEB at least
e^{-O(d)}
.

To conclude, the supporting argument for XQUATH, based on the conventional path integral formulation, does not hold if we consider a Pauli-basis path integral. This is because the contribution of each path becomes polarized (not equally weighted) and our subsystem algorithm is equivalent to outputting paths with weight
e^{-O(d)}
in the Pauli basis.

Details for the Neuroscience Part

Mathematical preliminaries

Definition .9

(Matrix norm)
.

For every positive semi-definite matrix
A
, the
A
-norm of a vector
\mathbf{x}
is defined as
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{A}=\sqrt{\mathbf{x}^{\top}A\mathbf{x}}
.

Note that when
A
is the identity matrix, then
A
-norm is simply the Euclidean norm.

Lemma .1

(Cauchy-Schwarz inequality)
.

Let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two vectors, we have
|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{y}|\leq\sqrt{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}}
.

Optimal Balanced SNNs Solves the Least Squares Problem

In this section, we give a complete proof for the convergence of optimal balanced SNN to the (non-negative) least squares problem as discussed in
Our results
,
Theorem 0.3
(with an informal version,
Theorem 0.1
), and
Theorem 0.4
(with an informal version,
Theorem 0.2
).

Recall that given a matrix
F\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
and a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the goal of the least squares problem is to find
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that minimizes
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}/2
. Here we consider the discrete and non-leaky optimal balanced SNNs (
Equation 32
):

\mathbf{v}(t+1)=-FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)+F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t

(88)

where
\mathbf{v}(0)
is instantiated to the all zero vector. For the other parameters, we require the threshold
\eta\geq\lambda_{\max}
and the timestep size
\Delta t\leq\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}{24\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}
, where
\lambda_{\min}
and
\lambda_{\max}
are the minimal and maximal (non-zero) eigenvalues of
FF^{\top}
. Here we restate
Theorem 0.3
which shows that the residual error
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{2}^{2}/2
converges to its minimum with rate
O(1/t)
.

See
0.3

Before proving the theorem, we first give some intuition about why the firing rate would converge to an optimal least squares solution. An important fact here is that the neurons’ potential
\mathbf{v}(t)
will remain bounded (specifically, we will show that the
(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}
-norm of
\mathbf{v}(t)
is bounded). Now, recall that the potential is affected by the constant external charging current
I\cdot\Delta t=F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t
and the spike
-FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}
. Intuitively, to ensure the potential being bounded, the constant external charging current needs to be balanced out by the spiking effect over time. Thus, in the limit, we should expect

FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}\approx F\mathbf{x}\,.

Namely, the average effect of the spike should roughly be the external charging for one unit of time. Let
\mathbf{x}_{F}
be the orthogonal projection of
\mathbf{x}
onto the orthogonal complement of the null space of
F
. Note that as
F\mathbf{x}=F\mathbf{x}_{F}
and
\mathbf{x}_{F}
lies in the range space of
F^{\top}
, this implies that
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\approx\mathbf{x}_{F}
. Moreover, the minimal residual error
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}/2
would be
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}/2
since
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}
always lies in the range space of
F^{\top}
. Thus, in the following, we focus on upper bounding
\|\mathbf{x}_{F}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)\|_{2}
instead.

In the following, we start with a lemma about the properties of matrix norm and then prove the boundedness of
\mathbf{v}(t)
. The proofs of these two lemmas are provided in the subsequent subsection. Finally, we use the “potential conservation argument” to prove
Theorem 0.3
.

Lemma .2
.

For every
F\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
, we have

1.

For every
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{FF^{\top}}=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}=\|FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}
.

2.

For every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\|F\mathbf{x}\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}=\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}
.

Lemma .3
.

For every time
t\in\mathbb{N}
, when the threshold
\eta\geq\lambda_{\max}
and the discretized step size
\Delta t<\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}{24\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}}
, we have
\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}\leq 2\sqrt{\kappa\eta n}
.

We now prove
Theorem 0.3
using
Lemma .3
.

Proof of
Theorem 0.3
.

The proof consists of two steps. First, using the dynamics of
\mathbf{v}(t)
to derive a connection between
\mathbf{v}(t)
and the residual error of the least squares problem. Second, use
Lemma .3
to upper bound the residual error.

Let us start with telescoping
Equation 88
from time 1 to time
t
,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbf{v}(s+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left(\mathbf{v}(s)-FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(s)+F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbf{v}(s)\right)-(t\cdot\Delta t)\cdot\left(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)-F\mathbf{x}\right)\,.

Note that the second equality uses the fact that
\mathbf{r}(t)=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbf{s}(s)
for non-leaky SNNs. Next, subtract
\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbf{v}(s)
on both sides and divide with
t\cdot\Delta t
. Now, we have established an elegant connection between the firing rate and the potential vector.

\frac{\mathbf{v}(t+1)-\mathbf{v}(1)}{t\cdot\Delta t}=F\mathbf{x}-FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)\,.

(89)

Note that the right-hand side of
Equation 89
is
F
multiplying the residual error
\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)
. To get rid of the
F
term, we apply the
(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}
-norm on the both sides of
Equation 89
. By
Lemma .2
and the fact that
\mathbf{x}_{F}
is the projection of
\mathbf{x}
on the range space of
F^{\top}
, we have

\frac{\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)-\mathbf{v}(1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}}{t\cdot\Delta t}=\|F\mathbf{x}-FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}=\|\mathbf{x}_{F}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)\|_{2}\,.

(90)

As a result, to upper bound the error
\|\mathbf{x}_{F}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)\|_{2}
, it suffices to upper bound
\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)-\mathbf{v}(1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}
. Moreover, as we take
\mathbf{v}(1)=\mathbf{0}
, now we only need to show
\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}
is bounded by some
\epsilon
multiplicative factor of
\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}
. As we take
\Delta t\leq\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}{24\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}}
and
\eta\geq\lambda_{\max}
,
Lemma .3
gives that
\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}\leq 2\sqrt{\kappa\eta n}
for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Combine this upper bound for
\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}
with
Equation 90
and the choice of
t\geq 2\sqrt{\kappa\eta n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}/(\epsilon\cdot\Delta t)
, we have

\|\mathbf{x}_{F}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}(t)\|_{2}\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}}{t\cdot\Delta t}\leq\frac{2\epsilon\sqrt{\kappa\eta n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}}{2\sqrt{\kappa\eta n}}=\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}\,.

(91)

∎

Proof of
Lemma .2

In the following, we prove two properties of the matrix norm. First, for every
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{FF^{\top}}=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}=\|FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}
. Second,
\|F\mathbf{x}\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}=\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}
.

1.

By definition, we have
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{FF^{\top}}=\sqrt{\mathbf{r}^{\top}FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}}=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}
. As for the second part of the equality, recall that if a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
lies in the range space of
FF^{\top}
, then

(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}(FF^{\top})\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}.

(92)

Let
\mathbf{r}_{F}
be the orthogonal projection of
\mathbf{r}
such that
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}=F^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F}
and
\mathbf{r}_{F}
lies in the range space of
FF^{\top}
. By
Equation 92
, we have

\displaystyle\|FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r})^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F})^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F})

\displaystyle=(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F})^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F}

\displaystyle=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F}\|_{2}^{2}=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

2.

Let
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a solution of
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{x}_{F}
where
\mathbf{x}_{F}
is the orthogonal projection of
\mathbf{x}
to the orthogonal complement of the null space of
F
. By
Equation 92
, we have

\displaystyle\|F\mathbf{x}\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(F\mathbf{x})^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}(F\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r})^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}(FF^{\top}\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}^{2}.

Proof of
Lemma .3

At timestep t, denote the set of neurons that exceeds the threshold as
\Gamma(t):=\{i\in[n]\,|\,|\mathbf{v}_{i}(t)|>\eta\}
. We have the following observation.

Lemma .4
.

For every
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\geq\eta\cdot|\Gamma(t)|
.

Proof.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{v}_{i}(t)\cdot\mathbf{s}_{i}(t)

(93)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\Gamma(t)}\mathbf{v}_{i}(t)\cdot\mbox{sign}(\mathbf{v}_{i}(t))=\sum_{i\in\Gamma(t)}|\mathbf{v}_{i}(t)|

(94)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in\Gamma(t)}\eta=\eta\cdot|\Gamma(t)|.

(95)

∎

Lemma .5
.

For every
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)=\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)
.

Proof.

First, note that
(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}FF^{\top}
is the orthogonal projection matrix for the range space of
FF^{\top}
. Next, by the dynamics of SNN and
\mathbf{v}(1)=\mathbf{0}
, we know that
\mathbf{v}(t)
is in the range space of
FF^{\top}
. Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the unique vector in the range space of
FF^{\top}
such that
\mathbf{v}(t)=FF^{\top}\mathbf{z}(t)
. Let
\mathbf{s}_{F}(t)
be the orthogonal projection of
\mathbf{s}(t)
onto the range space of
FF^{\top}
. Note that
FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)=FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}_{F}(t)
. By
Lemma .2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}^{\top}FF^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}_{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}^{\top}FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}_{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}^{\top}FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)=\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t).

∎

Finally, we are ready to prove
Lemma .3
.

Proof of
Lemma .3
.

The proof is based on an induction over the number of iterations
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
B=2\sqrt{\kappa\eta n}
, our goal is to show that
\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}\leq B
for every
t
when
\Delta t\leq\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}{24\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}}
. Clearly that
\|\mathbf{v}(1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}=0\leq B
. Now, assume that
\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}\leq B
, let us consider

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{v}(t)-FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)+F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}+\|FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)-F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle-2\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}\Big(FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)-F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\Big).

(96)

Expand the
\|FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)-F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}
term, by
Lemma .2
, we have

\displaystyle\|FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)-F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)-\mathbf{x}_{F}\cdot\Delta t\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\cdot\Delta t\|_{2}^{2}-2\mathbf{s}(t)^{\top}F\mathbf{x}_{F}\cdot\Delta t

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max}\cdot|\Gamma(t)|+\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\Delta t^{2}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}\cdot|\Gamma(t)|}\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}\cdot\Delta t.

(97)

The last inequality is from Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and the fact that
\|\mathbf{s}(t)\|^{2}_{2}=|\Gamma(t)|
. Next, by
Lemma .2
,
Lemma .4
,
Lemma .5
, Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, and the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle-2\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}\Big(FF^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)-F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\Big)

\displaystyle=-2\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)+2\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}\Big(F\mathbf{x}\cdot\Delta t\Big)

(98)

\displaystyle\leq-2\mathbf{v}(t)^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)+2\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}\cdot\Delta t

(99)

\displaystyle\leq-2\eta|\Gamma(t)|+2B\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}\cdot\Delta t.

(100)

Combine
Equation 96
,
Equation 97
,
Equation 100
, and induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}+(\lambda_{\max}-2\eta)\cdot|\Gamma(t)|

\displaystyle+(2\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}|\Gamma(t)|}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}+2B\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2})\cdot\Delta t+\|\mathbf{x}_{F}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\Delta t^{2}.

By the choice of
\eta,\Delta t
, and
B
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}-\lambda_{\max}\cdot|\Gamma(t)|+\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}\lambda_{\min}|\Gamma(t)|}}{6\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{6}+\frac{\lambda_{\min}}{144n}

(101)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}-\lambda_{\max}|\Gamma(t)|+\frac{\lambda_{\max}|\Gamma(t)|}{2}+\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}.

(102)

Now, there are two cases to consider:
\Gamma(t)
is empty or not. From (
Equation 102
) we can see that if
\Gamma(t)
is nonempty, then by the induction hypothesis,
\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}\leq\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}\leq B
. If
\Gamma(t)
is empty, then for any
i\in[n]
,
|\mathbf{u}_{i}(t)|\leq\eta
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}+\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}.

(103)

Since
|\Gamma(t)|=0
, we know that
\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
and thus

\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}^{2}\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\leq n\cdot\max_{i\in[n]}|\mathbf{v}_{i}(t)|^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}}\leq\frac{\eta^{2}n}{\lambda_{\min}}.

As a result, for the
|\Gamma(t)|=0
case, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}(t+1)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{v}(t)\|_{(FF^{\top})^{\dagger}}+\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}+\frac{B}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\kappa\eta n}+\frac{B}{2}\leq B.

We conclude that the induction holds and hence finish the proof of
Lemma .3
.
∎

Derivations of the dual programs

\ell_{1}
minimization

Recall that the (non-negative)
\ell_{1}
minimization problem is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\text{minimize}}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}

(
\ell_{1}
minimization)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle F^{\top}\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{x},\ \mathbf{r}\geq 0.

To derive the dual problem, we first write down the Lagrangian:

L(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{\lambda},\nu)=\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{\lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{x})

(104)

where we implicitly replace
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}
with
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}
as they are the same when
\mathbf{r}\geq 0
. Next, we derive the Lagrangian dual function:

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{\lambda},\mathbf{\nu})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbf{r}}L(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{\lambda},\mathbf{\nu})

(105)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbf{r}}\left\{[(\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})^{\top}+\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}F^{\top}]\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\right\}

(106)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\infty&\text{, if }(\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})^{\top}+\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}F^{\top}\neq 0\\
-\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}\mathbf{x}&\text{, if }(\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})^{\top}+\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}F^{\top}=0.\end{array}\right.

Finally, the dual problem is defined as the maximum of
g(\mathbf{\lambda},\mathbf{\nu})
subject to
\mathbf{\lambda}\geq 0
. As the first condition in
\ell_{1}
minimization
won’t lead to the minimizer, the dual problem is the following.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbf{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})^{\top}+\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}F^{\top}=0,\ \mathbf{\lambda}\geq 0.

Note that the condition in the above program is equivalent to
-\mathbf{\nu}^{\top}F^{\top}\leq 1
. By setting
\mathbf{u}=-\mathbf{\nu}
and slightly rearranging the equations, we can rewrite the dual program in the form as we saw in
Equation 34
.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle F\mathbf{u}\leq 1.

Lasso

Recall that the (non-negative) Lasso problem is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\text{minimize}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}+\beta\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}

(Lasso)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}\geq\mathbf{0}.

To derive the dual problem, we first write down the Lagrangian:

L(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{\lambda})=\frac{1}{2}\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}+\beta\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{\lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{r}

(109)

where we implicitly replace
\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}
with
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{r}
as they are the same when
\mathbf{r}\geq 0
. Next, we derive the Lagrangian dual function:

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbf{r}}L(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{\lambda})

(110)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbf{r}}\left\{(\beta\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})^{\top}\mathbf{r}+\frac{1}{2}\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}\right\}\,.

(111)

Note that the minimizer should be of the form
(F^{\dagger})^{\top}\mathbf{y}
where
F^{\dagger}
is the (right) pseudo-inverse of
F
. Thus we have

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{\lambda})=

\displaystyle\ \inf_{\mathbf{y}}\left\{(\beta\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})^{\top}(F^{\dagger})^{\top}\mathbf{y}+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}\right\}

(112)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{y}-(\mathbf{x}-F^{\dagger}(\beta\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda}))\|_{2}^{2}

(113)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\dagger}(\beta\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})\|_{2}^{2}

(114)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\dagger}(\beta\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})\|_{2}^{2}\,.

(115)

Finally, the dual problem is defined as the maximum of
g(\mathbf{\lambda})
subject to
\mathbf{\lambda}\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\dagger}(\beta\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\lambda}\geq 0\,.

Note that by setting
\mathbf{u}=F^{\dagger}(\beta\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{\lambda})/\beta
, the constraints become
F\mathbf{u}\leq\mathbf{1}
. Hence, we can rewrite the dual program in the form as we saw in
Equation 36
.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}-\beta\mathbf{u}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle F\mathbf{u}\leq 1\,.

Optimal Balanced SNNs Solves the
\ell_{1}
Minimization Problem

See
0.4

Two remarks on the statement of
Theorem 0.4
. First, we consider the
continuous SNN
instead of the discrete SNN, which is of interest for simulation on classical computer. In discrete SNN, the
step size
is some non-negligible
\Delta t>0
instead of
dt
. The main reason for considering continuous SNN is that this significantly simplify the proof by avoiding a huge amount of nasty calculations. We suspect that the proof idea would hold for discrete SNN with discretization parameter
\Delta t\leq\Delta t(\frac{\gamma(F)}{n\cdot\lambda_{\max}})
for some polynomial
\Delta t(\cdot)
.
Second, the parameters in
Theorem 0.4
have not been optimized and we believe all the dependencies can be improved. Since the parameters highly affect the efficiency of SNN as an algorithm for
\ell_{1}
minimization problem, we pose it as an interesting open problem to study what are the best dependencies one can get.

Overview of the proof for
Theorem 0.4

The proof of
Theorem 0.4
consists of two main steps as mentioned in the previous subsection. The first step argues that the dual SNN
\mathbf{u}(t)
would converge to the neighborhood of the optimal dual solution
\mathbf{u}^{\textsf{OPT}}
. The second step is connecting the dual solution (
i.e.,
the dual SNN) to the primal solution (
i.e.,
the firing rate).

In the first step, we try to identify a
potential function
23
23

23

Potential function is widely used in the analysis of many gradient-descent based algorithm. In the theory of dynamical systems, sometimes people use the term “Lyapunov function”. The difficulty lies in the search of a good potential function for the algorithm.
that captures how close is
\mathbf{u}(t)
to the optimal dual solution
\mathbf{u}^{\textsf{OPT}}
.
It turns out that this is not an easy task since the effect of spikes makes the behavior of dual SNN very non-monotone. We conquer the difficulty via a technique that we call
ideal coupling
(see
Definition .13
and Figure
46
). The idea is to associate the dual SNN
\mathbf{u}(t)
with an
ideal SNN

\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
for every
t\geq 0
such that the ideal SNN would have
smoother
behavior comparing to the spiking phenomenon in the dual SNN. We will formally define the ideal SNN in Section
Ideal coupling
. There are two advantages of using ideal SNN instead of handling dual SNN directly:
(i) Ideal SNN is smoother than dual SNN in the sense that it would not change after spikes (see
Lemma .9
). Further, by introducing some auxiliary processes (
i.e.,
the auxiliary SNNs defined in
Definition .15
), we are able to identify a potential function that is strictly improving at any moment and measures how well the dual SNN has been solving the
\ell_{1}
minimization problem (see
Lemma .12
).
(ii) ideal SNN is naturally associated with an
ideal solution
(defined in
Definition .14
) which is easier to analyze than the firing rate. Using these good properties of ideal SNN, we can prove in
Lemma .15
that the
\ell_{2}
residual error of the ideal solution will converge to
0
.

After we are able to show the convergence of the
\ell_{2}
residual error in
Lemma .15
, we move to the second step where the goal is showing that the
\ell_{1}
norm of the solution is also small. We look at the KKT conditions of the
\ell_{1}
minimization problem and observe that the primal and dual solutions of SNN satisfy the KKT conditions of a
perturbed
program of the
\ell_{1}
minimization problem. Finally, combine tools from perturbation theory, we can upper bound the
\ell_{1}
error of the ideal solution by its
\ell_{2}
residual error in
Lemma .16
.

Theorem
0.4
then follows from
Lemma .15
and
Lemma .16
with some special cares on how to transform everything for ideal solution to the firing rate. See Figure
for an overall structure of the proof for
Theorem 0.4
.

In the rest of this section, we are going to start from some definitions on the
nice conditions
we need for the input matrix in Section
Some nice conditions on the input matrix
. Next, we define the ideal coupling in Section
Ideal coupling
and prove
Lemma .9
and
Lemma .12
in Section
Ideal SNN remains unchanged after firing spikes
and Section
Strict convergence of ideal SNN and auxiliary SNNs
respectively. Finally, we wrap up the proof for
Theorem 0.4
in Section
The convergence of dual SNN
.

Some nice conditions on the input matrix

We need some
nice conditions
for the input matrix as follows.

Definition .10

(non-degeneracy)
.

Let
F\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
where
m\leq n
. We say
F
is non-degenerate if for any size
m\times m
submatrix of
F
has full rank. For any
\gamma>0
, we say
F
is
\gamma
-non-degenerate if for any
\Gamma\subseteq[n]
,
|\Gamma|=m
, and
i\in\Gamma
,
\|F_{i}-\Pi_{F_{\Gamma\backslash\{i\}}}F_{i}\|_{2}\geq\gamma
where
\Pi_{F_{\Gamma\backslash\{i\}}}\mathbf{v}
is the projection of
\mathbf{v}
onto subspace spanned by
\{F_{j}:\ j\in\Gamma\backslash\{i\}\|\}
for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Note that if
F
is non-degenerate, then for any
S\subseteq[n]
and
|S|=m
and
\mathbf{x}\in\{-1,1\}^{m}
, there exists an unique solution
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to
F_{S}^{\top}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{x}
where
F_{S}
is the submatrix of
F
restricted to columns in
S
. We call such
\mathbf{v}
a
vertex
of the polytope
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
. Note that in this definition, a vertex might not lie in
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
. An important parameter for future analysis is the minimum distance between two distinct vertices of
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
.

Definition .11

(nice input matrix)
.

Let
F\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
and
\gamma\geq 0
. We say
F
is
\gamma
-nice if all of the following conditions hold.

(1)

F
is
\gamma
-non-degenerate.

(2)

The distance between any two distinct vertices of
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
is at least
\gamma
.

(3)

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\{-1,1\}^{m}
,
\Gamma\subseteq[n]
, and
|\Gamma|=m
, let
\mathbf{x}=(F_{\Gamma}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{x}
. For any
i\in[m]
,
|\mathbf{x}_{i}|\geq\gamma
.

Define
\gamma(F)
to be the largest
\gamma
such that
F
is
\gamma
-nice. We say
F
is
nice
if
\gamma(F)>0
.

To motivate the definition of niceness, the following lemma shows that the
\ell_{1}
minimization problem defined by matrix
F
has unique solution if
\gamma(F)>0
.

Lemma .6
.

Let
F\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
. If
\gamma(F)>0
, then for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the
\ell_{1}
minimization problem for
(F,\mathbf{x})
has an unique solution.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by contradiction. Suppose there exists
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that there are two distinct solutions
\mathbf{x}_{1}\neq\mathbf{x}_{2}
to the
\ell_{1}
minimization problem for
(F,\mathbf{x})
. Let
\mathbf{v}^{*}
be the optimal solution of the dual program as in equation
. By the complementary slackness in the KKT condition, for any optimal solution
\mathbf{x}
to the primal program,
\text{supp}(\mathbf{x})\subseteq\{i\in[n]:\ |F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}^{*}|=1\}
. Let
S=\{i\in[n]:\ |F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}^{*}|=1\}
, then both
\mathbf{x}_{1}
and
\mathbf{x}_{2}
are solution to
F_{S}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{S}
where
F_{S}
and
\mathbf{x}_{S}
are restrictions to index set
S
. As
\gamma(F)>0
, we have
|S|\leq m
. By the non-degeneracy of
F
,
F_{S}
has full rank and thus
F_{S}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{S}
has unique solution. That is,
\mathbf{x}_{1}=\mathbf{x}_{2}
, which is a contradiction.

We conclude that if
F
is non-degenerate and
\gamma(F)>0
, then for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the
\ell_{1}
minimization problem for
(A,\mathbf{x})
has unique solution.
∎

In general, it is easy to find a matrix
F
such that
\gamma(F)=0
. However, we would like to argue that most of the matrices are actually nice.
The following lemma shows that random matrix
F
sampled from the
rotational symmetry model (RSM)
is nice. In RSM, each column of
F
is an uniform vector on the unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Note that such matrix for
\ell_{1}
minimization problem is commonly used in practice such as compressed sensing.

Lemma .7
.

Let
F\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
be a random matrix samples from RSM, then
\gamma(F)>0
with high probability.

Proof.

First, we show that
F
is non-degenerate with high probability. For any
\Gamma\subseteq[n]
and
i\in\Gamma
, denote the event where
F_{i}=\Pi_{F_{\Gamma\backslash\{i\}}}F_{i}
as
E_{\Gamma,i}
. Note that this event is measured zero for all choice of
\Gamma
and
i
and thus by union bound, we have
F
being non-degenerate with high probability. For the other two properties, similar arguments hold.
∎

We remark that giving a lower bound in terms of
m
and
n
for
\gamma(F)
would result in a better asymptotic bound for our main theorem and could have applications in other problems too. Since the goal of this paper is giving a provable analysis, we do not intend to optimize the parameter. Note that for
F
sampled from RSM,
\gamma(F)
has an inverse exponential lower bound directly from union bound when
n
and
m
are polynomially related. As for upper bound, there are inverse quasi-polynomial upper bound if
n\geq\textsf{polylog}(m)\cdot m
and inverse exponential upper bound if
n\geq m^{1+\Omega(1)}
as pointed out by the anonymous reviewer from ITCS 2019. We leave it as an open question to understand the correct asymptotic behavior of
\gamma(F)
when
F
is sampled from RSM.

Ideal coupling

Ideal coupling is a technique to keeping track of the dual SNN
\mathbf{u}(t)
by associating any point in the dual polytope to a point in a smaller polytope. Concretely, let
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}=\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\ \|F\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}
be the dual polytope and
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
be the
ideal polytope
where
\tau\in(0,1)
is an important parameter that will be properly chosen
24
24

24

The choice of
\tau
depends on
F
and
1
and will be discussed later.
in the end of the proof. Observe that
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}\subsetneq\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
. The idea of ideal coupling is associating each
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
with a point
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
in
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
. In the analysis, we will then focus on the dynamics of
\mathbf{u}^{\text{idael}}
instead of that of
\mathbf{u}
.

Before we formally define the coupling, we have to define a
partition
of
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
with respect to
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
as follows.

Definition .12

(partition of
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
be defined as above. For each
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}\in\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
, define

S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}=\{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}+\mathcal{C}_{F,\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{ideal})}\}\cap\mathcal{P}_{F,1}.

where
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})=\{i\in[\pm n]:\ \langle F_{i},\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}\rangle=1-\tau\}
is the active walls of
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{F,\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{ideal})}=\{\sum_{i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})}a_{i}F_{i},\ \forall a_{i}\geq 0\}
is the cone spanned by the column of
F
indexed by
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})
.

Consider the example where
A=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\tau\in(0,1)
. The dual polytope (resp. ideal polytope) is the square with vertices in the form
(\pm 1,\pm 1)
(resp.
(\pm 1-\tau,\pm 1-\tau)
). For a arbitrary
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}=(x,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
, let us see what
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}
is:

•

When
|x|,|y|<1-\tau
,
i.e.,

\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
strictly lies inside
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
,
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})=\emptyset
and thus
C_{F,\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})}=\emptyset
. Namely,
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
.

•

When
|x|=1-\tau
and
|y|<1-\tau
,
i.e.,

\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
lies on an edge of the ideal polytope,
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})=\{\text{sgn}(x)\cdot 1\}
and thus
C_{F,\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})}=\{(a,0):\ a\geq 0\}
. Namely,
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}=\{(a,y):\ a\in[1-\tau,1]\}
.

•

When
|x|<1-\tau
and
|y|=1-\tau
,
i.e.,

\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
lies on an edge of the ideal polytope,
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})=\{\text{sgn}(y)\cdot 2\}
and thus
C_{F,\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})}=\{(0,b):\ b\geq 0\}
. Namely,
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}=\{(x,b):\ b\in[1-\tau,1]\}
.

•

When
|x|=|y|=1-\tau
,
i.e.,

\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
lies on a vertex of the ideal polytope,
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})=\{\text{sgn}(x)\cdot 1,\text{sgn}(y)\cdot 2\}
and thus
C_{F,\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})}=\{(a,b):\ a,b\geq 0\}
. Namely,
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}=\{(a,b):\ a,b\in[1-\tau,1]\}
.

The following lemma checks that
Definition .12
does give a partition for
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
.

Lemma .8
.

\{S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}\}_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}\in\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}}
is a partition for
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
.

Proof of
Lemma .8
.

The proof is basically doing case analysis and using some basic properties from linear algebra. See Section
Proofs for the properties of ideal and auxiliary SNN
for details.
∎

Definition .13

(ideal coupling)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
be defined as above. For any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
, define
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(\mathbf{v})
be the unique
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
such that
\mathbf{v}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}
. We denote
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(\mathbf{v})
as
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
when the context is clear. Specifically, for any
t\geq 0
, we denote
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(\mathbf{v}(t))
as the ideal SNN at time
t
.

See Figure
46
for an example of the ideal coupling.

Figure 46:
This is an example of ideal coupling in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
where
\tau=0.4
,
F_{1}=[1\ 0]^{\top}
,
F_{2}=[0\ 1]^{\top}
, and
F_{3}=[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}]^{\top}
. The dots (
i.e.,

\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3}
) are dual SNN and the stars (
i.e.,

\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\text{ideal}},\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\text{ideal}},\mathbf{v}_{3}^{\text{ideal}}
) are the corresponding ideal SNN. The whole gray area is the dual polytope
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
and the gray area in the middle is the ideal polytope
\mathcal{P}_{1-\tau}
.

Note that
Definition .13
is well-defined due to
Lemma .8
. With the ideal coupling, we are then switching to analyze the
ideal SNN

\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
instead of the dual SNN
\mathbf{u}(t)
. In the following, we are going to show that the ideal SNN is indeed tractable for analysis, though it is highly non-trivial and is very sensitive to the choice of parameters.

To show the convergence of ideal SNN, we need a notion to measure how close
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
and the optimal point is. To do so, we define the
ideal solution
of ideal SNN at time
t
as follows.

Definition .14

(ideal solution)
.

For any
t\geq 0
, define the ideal solution
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
at time
t
as

\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\geq 0,\\
\mathbf{x}_{i}=0,\ \forall i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))\end{subarray}}\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}.

Also, let
\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))=\{i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)):\ \mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\neq 0\}
to be the set of super active neurons.

In the later proof, we need one more definition on a variant of ideal SNN called the
super SNN
. Similar to
Definition .14
, we define the super ideal SNN
\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}(t)
as the projection of
\mathbf{v}(t)
to the ideal polytope
without
those non-super ideal neurons. Formally, define
\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}(t)
be the unique solution of the following equations:
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}(t)-F_{\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}\mathbf{z}
and
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}=1-\tau
for each
i\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
. See Figure
47
for example. Note that the uniqueness of the solution is guaranteed by the non-degeneracy of
F
.

Figure 47:
This is an example of
\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
where
\tau=0.4
,
F_{1}=[0\ -1]^{\top}
,
F_{2}=[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ -\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}]^{\top}
,
\mathbf{x}=[1\ 0]^{\top}
, and
\mathbf{u}=[0.4\ -0.9]^{\top}
. The light gray area is the ideal polytope and the dark gray area is the dual polytope. In this example, we have
\Gamma(\mathbf{u})=\{1,2\}
while
\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\{2\}
. As a result,
\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}
is defined as the projection of
\mathbf{u}
onto the ideal polytope that only contains neuron
2
.

It is indeed unclear why we need these definitions at this stage of the proof. It would be clearer why we need them in the next two subsections once we go into the main analysis. Before we move on to more details, see Figure
46
and Figure
47
again to familiarize with the definitions.

Ideal SNN remains unchanged after firing spikes

In this subsection, we are going to prove the following important lemma saying that the dual SNN would not change its ideal SNN after firing spikes.

Lemma .9

(ideal SNN remains unchanged after firing spikes)
.

There exists a polynomial
\alpha(\cdot)
such that if
F
is nice and
0<\alpha\leq\alpha(\frac{\tau\cdot\gamma(F)}{n\cdot\lambda_{\max}})
, then
\mathbf{u}(t)-\alpha F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
for each
t\geq 0
.

Proof of
Lemma .9
.

First, note that for each
\mathbf{u}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
, by the property of dual polytope, there exists an unique
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{|\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|}
such that
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\mathbf{z}
where
\mathbf{z}
can be thought of as the
coordinates
of
\mathbf{u}
in
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
. With this concept in mind, it is then sufficient to show that whenever neuron
i
fires,
\mathbf{z}_{i}>\alpha
. The reason is that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}(t)-\alpha F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\mathbf{z}-\sum_{i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))}\alpha F_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+\sum_{i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))\backslash\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))}\mathbf{z}_{i}F_{i}+\sum_{i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))}(\mathbf{z}_{i}-\alpha)F_{i}.

(116)

As a result, if
\mathbf{z}_{i}-\alpha>0
for every
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))
, then we have
\mathbf{u}(t)-\alpha F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
because every new coordinates are still non-negative. See Figure
48
for an example.

Figure 48:
This is an example of
coordinates
of
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
where
\tau=0.4
,
F_{1}=[0\ -1]^{\top}
and
F_{2}=[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ -\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}]^{\top}
. The light gray area is the ideal polytope and the dark gray area is the dual polytope. In this example, the dot lines are the
level set
of each coordinates in
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}
. For instance, the
\mathbf{u}
in the figure has coordinate
\mathbf{z}=[0.1\ 0.2]^{\top}
and thus we have
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}+F^{\top}\mathbf{z}
.

Claim .2
.

There exists a polynomial
\alpha(\cdot)
such that when
0<\alpha\leq\textsf{poly}(\frac{\tau\cdot\gamma(F)}{n\cdot\lambda_{\max}})
and
\mathbf{u}(t)=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\mathbf{z}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
for some
t\geq 0
, if
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))
, then
\mathbf{z}_{i}>\alpha
.

Proof of Claim
.2
.

The proof consists of two steps. First, we are going to show that for any
t\geq 0
,
\mathbf{u}(t)
is close to
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
. Concretely, if
\alpha\leq\frac{\tau}{m}
, then
\|\mathbf{u}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\tau\lambda_{\max}
. Second, we are going to show that once we pick
\alpha
small enough, then for any
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
, the wall
W_{i}
is far away from the
\alpha
-level set in
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
. Thus, whenever neuron
i
fires,
\mathbf{z}_{i}>\alpha
.

The first step is a key observation that the distance between
\mathbf{u}(t)
and
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
would not increase after the neurons fire spikes. The main reason is that neuron
i
fires at time
t
if and only if
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}(t)>1
. As a result,

\displaystyle\|\left(\mathbf{u}(t)-\alpha F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\right)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \|\mathbf{u}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha^{2}\|F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\|_{2}^{2}-2\alpha\left(F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\right)^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \|\mathbf{u}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha^{2}\|F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\|_{2}^{2}-2\alpha\sum_{i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))}F_{i}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|\mathbf{u}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha^{2}|\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))|^{2}-2\alpha\tau|\Gamma(\mathbf{u}(t))|\,.

That is, if
\alpha\leq\frac{\tau}{m}
, then
\|\left(\mathbf{u}(t)-\alpha F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\right)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
would not increase after some neurons fire. Furthermore, the longest distance between
\mathbf{u}(t)
and
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
would then be
\tau\lambda_{\max}
.

The second step is rather complicated. Let us start with some definitions. Recall that for any
i\in[\pm n]
, the wall
i
is defined as
W_{i}=\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\ F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}=1\}
. Now, define the
\alpha
-level set of
i
in
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
as

L_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),i,\alpha}=\{\mathbf{u}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}:\ \mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\mathbf{z},\ \mathbf{z}_{i}=\alpha\}.

That is,
L_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),i,\alpha}
consists of the set of points in
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
that has the
i
-th coordinate to be
\alpha
.

Claim .3

(furtherest point in
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}
)
.

For any
t\geq 0
, let
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}
be the unique point
\mathbf{u}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
such that for any
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
,
F_{i}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\right)=\tau
. Then, we have
\|\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}=\max_{\mathbf{u}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}}\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
.

Proof of Claim
.3
.

Let us prove by contradiction. Suppose
\mathbf{u}^{*}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
such that
\|\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}<\|\mathbf{u}^{*}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
. To simplify the notations, let
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
and
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{*}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
.

By definition, we have
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}=\tau
for all
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
and
\mathbf{u}=F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{\Gamma}
for some
\mathbf{z}_{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. On the other hand, we also have
0\leq F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}\leq\tau
for all
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
.

Now, look at the quantity
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\right)
. Note that since
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}>\|\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\|_{2}
, we have
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\right)>0
. Also, for any
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
, we have
F_{i}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\right)\leq 0
. Using the fact that
\mathbf{u}=F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{\Gamma}
for some
\mathbf{z}_{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

\displaystyle 0<\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}_{\Gamma}^{\top}F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}_{\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{u}\leq 0,

where
\mathbf{u}=F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}^{|\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|}
. That is, we reach a contradiction and thus
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma}\|_{2}
and we conclude that
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}
is the furtherest point from
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
in
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
.
∎

Claim .4

(intersection of wall and
\alpha
-level set is far)
.

When
0<\alpha\leq\tau^{2}\cdot\gamma(F)^{3}
, for any
t\geq 0
and
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
, we have

\min_{\mathbf{u}:\ \mathbf{u}\in W_{i}\cap L_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),i,\alpha}}\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}>\|\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}.

Proof of Claim
.4
.

First, let us write
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+\sum_{i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}\mathbf{z}_{i}F_{i}
where
\mathbf{z}_{i}\geq\tau\cdot\gamma(F)
by
Definition .11
. Furthermore, for any
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
, we have

\text{dist}\left(\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))},\text{span}(F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))\backslash\{i\}})\right)\geq|\mathbf{z}_{i}|\cdot\text{dist}\left(F_{i},\text{span}(F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))\backslash\{i\}})\right)\geq\tau\cdot\gamma(F)^{2},

where the last inequality follows
Definition .11
. Namely, if we pick
0<\alpha<\tau^{2}\cdot\gamma(F)^{3}
, then

\text{dist}\left(\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))},L_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),i,\alpha}\right)>0

and
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}\in\text{Cone}(F_{i},L_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),i,\alpha})
because
\mathbf{z}_{i}\geq\gamma(F)
. Finally, observe that for any
\mathbf{u}\in W_{i}\cap L_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),i,\alpha}
, we have
\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}\right)>0
. This completes the proof of Claim
.4
.
∎

Combine Claim
.3
and Claim
.4
, we know that when neuron
i
fires, the corresponding coordinate
\mathbf{z}_{i}
will be at least
\alpha
. This completes the proof of Claim
.2
.
∎

Now,
Lemma .9
follows from Claim
.2
and equation
Equation 116
.
∎

Strict convergence of ideal SNN and auxiliary SNNs

In this subsection, the goal is to characterize the dynamics of both ideal and auxiliary SNN. Before defining auxiliary SNN, let us first see the following lemma about the dynamics of ideal SNN.

Lemma .10

(dynamics of ideal SNN)
.

If
F
is nice, then for any
t\geq 0
, we have

\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt)=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+\left(\mathbf{x}-\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}\right)dt.

Proof of
Lemma .10
.

We consider two cases: (i) there is no neuron fires any spike and (ii) there is a neuron fires a spike.

Case (i)
: By
Definition .13
,
\mathbf{u}(t)=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}+F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}^{\top}\mathbf{z}
for some
\mathbf{z}\geq 0
. Also, rewrite the updates
\mathbf{x}
as

\mathbf{x}=\left(\mathbf{x}-\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}\right)+\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}.

First,
F_{i}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{x}-\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}\right)=0
for each
i\in\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
. Next, since there is no neuron fires at time
t
, observe that
\mathbf{u}(t)+\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
. Finally, since
\mathbf{x}-\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}
is orthogonal to the subspace spanned by the active neurons, we then have
\mathbf{u}(t)+\mathbf{x}dt\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+(\mathbf{x}-\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x})dt}
.

Case (ii)
: To handle spikes, the idea is to focus on the spike term first, and once
\mathbf{u}(t)
goes back to the interior of the dual polytope, then it becomes case (i). Here, we use an assumption that if there are some neurons fire at time
t
and they trigger consecutive firing, we add the external charging
after
the consecutive firing. As a result, it suffices to show that
\mathbf{u}(t)-\alpha F^{\top}\mathbf{s}(t)\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)}
, which immediately follows from
Lemma .9
.

We conclude that for any
t\geq 0
,
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt)=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+\left(\mathbf{x}-\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}\right)dt
.
∎

From
Lemma .10
, one can see that the improvement of ideal SNN is not proportional to the residual error when the
\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}}\mathbf{x}\neq F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
. As a result, we have to design a bunch of
auxiliary SNN
to make sure that at least one of them has improvement proportional to the residual error. The auxiliary SNNs
\{\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\}_{d\in[m-1]}
is defined as follows.

Definition .15

(auxiliary SNNs)
.

For each
t\geq 0
, and
d\in[m-1]
, define
\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}(0)=\mathbf{0}
and

\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t+dt)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)+F^{\top}\left(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\right)dt&\text{, if }|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|=d\\
&\text{ and }|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|=d,\\
\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}(t+dt)&\text{, if }|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|=d\\
&\text{ and }|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|\neq d,\\
\mathbf{u}_{d}^{\text{auxiliary}}(t)&\text{, else}.\end{array}\right.

The auxiliary SNNs have the following important property that is crucial in the proof of the
Lemma .12
which gives the strict improvement guarantee.

Lemma .11

(Auxiliary SNNs jump)
.

Suppose
F
is nice and
\tau=O(\frac{\gamma(F)}{n^{2}\cdot\lambda_{\max}^{2}})
. For any
t>0
and
d\in[m-1]
, if
|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|\neq|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|=d
, then
\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t+dt)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\right)>0
.

Proof of
Lemma .11
.

By the definition of auxiliary SNNs, we have three observations. First,
\|\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t+dt)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}=\|\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}(t+dt)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}=O(\tau\cdot n\cdot\lambda_{\max})
. Second, there exists
0\leq t^{\prime}<t
such that
\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)=\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}(t^{\prime})
and
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t^{\prime}))\neq\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
. That is, we also have
\|\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t^{\prime})\|_{2}=\|\mathbf{u}^{\text{super}}(t^{\prime})-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}=O(\tau\cdot n\cdot\lambda_{\max})
. Finally, since
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t^{\prime}))\neq\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
, by
Lemma .10
, we have
\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t^{\prime})\right)=\Omega(\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\cdot\frac{\gamma(F)}{n\cdot\lambda_{\max}})
. Combine the three we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t+dt)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t^{\prime})\right)-O(\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\cdot\tau\cdot n\cdot\lambda_{\max})

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\cdot\frac{\lambda(F)}{n\cdot\lambda_{\max}})-O(\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\cdot\tau\cdot n\cdot\lambda_{\max})>0,

where the last inequality holds when we pick
\tau=O(\frac{\gamma(F)}{n^{2}\lambda_{\max}^{2}})
.
∎

Now, we are able to prove the main lemma about identifying a potential function that is strictly improving as long as
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
is not the optimal solution for
\ell_{1}
minimization problem.

Lemma .12

(strict improvement)
.

For any
t>0
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+\sum_{d\in[m-1]}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\right)\geq\mathbf{x}^{\top}F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t).

Proof of
Lemma .12
.

The proof is based on case analysis on the size of
|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|
. We consider three cases:

(i)

\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))=\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
,

(ii)

\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))\subsetneq\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
and
|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|=|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|
, and

(iii)

\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))\subsetneq\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
and
|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|\neq|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|
.

In each case, we are going to show that at least one of
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
or
\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)
for some
d\in[m-1]
has the desired improvement. Also, we need to show that all of them would not get worse. Formally, we state it as the following claim.

Claim .5
.

For every
t>0
and
d\in[m-1]
, we have
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\geq 0
.

Proof of Claim
.5
.

From
Lemma .10
, we already have
\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\geq 0
. For any
d\in[m-1]
, consider three cases as in
Definition .15
.

If
|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|=|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|=d
, then
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)=\mathbf{x}^{\top}F^{\top}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t))\geq 0
.

If
|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))|\neq|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|=d
, then by
Lemma .11
we have

\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t+dt)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\right)>0

and thus
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\geq 0
.

Finally, when none of the above happens, we simply have
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)=0
.
∎

With Claim
.5
, it suffices to show that at least one of
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
or
\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)
for some
d\in[m-1]
has the desired improvement in all of the above three cases.

Case (i)
: In this case,
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=\Pi_{F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))}\mathbf{x}}
. Thus, by
Lemma .10
, we have
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\right)
.

Case (ii)
: In this case, let
d=|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|
. By
Definition .15
, we have
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}(t)=\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\right)
.

Case (iii)
: In this case, let
d=|\Gamma^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t+dt))|
. By
Lemma .11
, we have

\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t+dt)-\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\right)>0

and thus
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\geq\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\right)
.

This completes the proof of
Lemma .12
.
∎

Finally, before we go into the final proof for
Theorem 0.4
, we need the following lemma about some properties about the ideal solution defined in
Definition .14
.

Lemma .13

(properties of ideal solution)
.

For any
t\geq 0
, we have the following.

1.

\mathbf{x}^{\top}F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}
,

2.

\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}=\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}
, and

Proof of
Lemma .13
.

The lemma is directly followed by the following property of conic projection. For any
F\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
\Gamma\subseteq[\pm n]
be a valid set, we have
\mathbf{x}^{\top}F\mathbf{x}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}=\|F\mathbf{x}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}
. In the following, we are going to first prove this property of conic projection and then use it to prove the lemma.

Let us rewrite the definition of conic projection as an optimization program.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\text{minimize}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}

(117)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{j}\geq 0,\ j\in\Gamma,

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{i}=0,\ i,-i\notin\Gamma.

Let
\mathbf{y}
be the dual variable of
Equation 117
and
\mathbf{y}^{*}
be the optimal dual value, the Lagrangian of
Equation 117
is

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{r},

and its gradient is

\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{r})=FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}-F\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}.

By the KKT condition, we know that the optimal primal solution
\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}
and the optimal dual solution
\mathbf{y}^{*}
make the gradient of the Lagrangian diminish.

\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma})=FF^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}-F\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}^{*}=0,

(118)

and the complementary slackness

\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{y}^{*}=0.

(119)

By
Equation 118
and
Equation 119
, we have

(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma})^{\top}(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}-\mathbf{x})=0.

As a result,
\mathbf{x}^{\top}F^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}=\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}_{F,\mathbf{x},\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}
.

This completes the proof of
Lemma .13
.
∎

The convergence of dual SNN

In this subsection, we are going to prove the main convergence theorem of the dual SNN using ideal and auxiliary SNN. The following lemma says that at least one of ideal SNN or auxiliary SNN improves at each step.

The following lemma shows the monotonicity of the residual error
\|\mathbf{x}^{\text{ideal}}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}
.

Lemma .14

(monotonicity of residual error)
.

There exists a polynomial
\alpha(\cdot)
such that when
0<\alpha\leq\alpha(\frac{\gamma(F)}{n\cdot\lambda_{\max}})
, we have
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
is non-increasing and
\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
is non-decreasing in
t
.

Proof of
Lemma .14
.

Consider two cases.

(1)

When there is a new index joins the active set. Clearly that
\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
won’t decrease since the new cone contains the old one. By
Lemma .13
, we know that
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
is non-increasing.

(2)

When there is an index leaves the the active set. Without loss of generality, assume
j\in[\pm n]
leaves the active set. In the following, we want to show that
\mathbf{x}^{\text{ideal}}_{|j|}(t)=0
. As the direction of
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
is
\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
, it means that
F_{j}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t))<0
. Suppose
\mathbf{x}^{\text{ideal}}_{|j|}(t)\neq 0
for contradiction. Since
j
was in the active set, it is the case that
\mathbf{x}^{\text{ideal}}_{j}(t)>0
. Take
0<\epsilon<\min\{\mathbf{x}^{\text{ideal}}_{j}(t)/2,-(\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t))^{\top}F_{j}/\|F_{j}\|_{2}\}
and define
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)-\epsilon\cdot F_{j}/\|F_{j}\|_{2}
. Note that
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
lies in the original active cone. Observe that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)-\epsilon\cdot F_{j}/\|F_{j}\|_{2}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\epsilon\cdot F_{j}/\|F_{j}\|_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon\cdot(\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t))^{\top}F_{j}/\|F_{j}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}-2\epsilon^{2}

\displaystyle<\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}

which contradicts to the optimality of
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
since
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
is also a feasible solution. We conclude that
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}_{j}(t)=0
. As a result,
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
remains the same and
\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
won’t decrease.

∎

The next lemma upper bounds the
\ell_{2}
residual error of
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
.

Lemma .15

(convergence of residual error)
.

There exists a polynomial
\alpha(\cdot)
such that when
0<\alpha\leq\alpha(\frac{\gamma(F)}{n\cdot\lambda_{\max}})
, we have for any
\epsilon>0
, when
t\geq\frac{m\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}}{\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}
,
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}
.

Proof of
Lemma .15
.

Assume the statement is wrong, i.e.,
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}>\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}
. Then by
Lemma .14
, for any
0\leq s\leq t
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(s)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\|F\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(s)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\|F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}>\epsilon^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
t\geq\frac{\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}}{\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}
, by
Lemma .12
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+\sum_{d\in[m-1]}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{x}^{\top}d\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)+\sum_{d\in[m-1]}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{x}^{\top}d\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)

\displaystyle>t\cdot\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\geq m\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}},

which is a contradiction to the optimality of
\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}
since
\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t),\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{auxiliary}}_{d}(t)\leq\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}
for all
d\in[m-1]
. As a result, we conclude that
\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}
.
∎

Finally, the following lemma shows that the
\ell_{1}
error of
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
can be upper bounded by the
\ell_{2}
error via the strong duality of
\ell_{1}
minimization problem and perturbation trick.

Lemma .16

(convergence of
\ell_{1}
error)
.

For any
t\geq 0
,

\left|\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}-\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda_{\min}}}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}

(120)

Proof sketch.

The proof of
Lemma .16
consists of two steps. First, we show that the primal and the dual solution pair of ideal SNN at time
t
is the optimal solution pair of a
perturbed
\ell_{1}
minimization problem
defined as shifting the
\mathbf{x}
in the constraint
F\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}
to
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
. See
Equation 121
for the definition of the perturbed program. Next, by the standard perturbation theorem from optimization, we can upper bound
\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}
with the distance between the original program and the perturbed program. Specifically, the difference induced by the perturbation is related to the
\ell_{2}
norm of the differnce between
\mathbf{x}
and
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
, which is exactly the residual error. As a result, we know that the difference between the optimal value of the original
\ell_{1}
minimization program and that of the perturbed program will converge to 0. Namely, we yield a convergence of
\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}
to
\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}
. See Section
Proofs for the convergent analysis of solving
\ell_{1}
minimization
for more details.
∎

Finally, we can prove the main theorem in this section as follows.

Proof of
Theorem 0.4
.

Pick
t_{0}=\Theta(\frac{m\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}{\epsilon\cdot\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}}})
. By
Lemma .15
, for any
t\geq t_{0}
, we can upper bound the
\ell_{2}
residual error by

\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{\min}}{n}}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{10}\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}.

Next, by
Lemma .16
, we can then upper bound the
\ell_{1}
error by

\left|\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}-\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda_{\min}}}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{10}\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}\,.

Now, the only thing left is connecting the ideal solution
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
to the firing rate
\mathbf{r}(t)
. First, divide
\mathbf{r}(t)
into two parts: the firing rate
\mathbf{r}^{[0,t_{0}]}
before time
t_{0}
and the firing rate
\mathbf{x}^{(t_{0},t]}
from time
t_{0}
to
t
. That is,
\mathbf{r}(t)=\frac{t_{0}}{t}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{[0,t_{0}]}+\frac{t-t_{0}}{t}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{(t_{0},t]}
.

Note that after
t_{0}\geq\Omega(\frac{m\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}{\epsilon\cdot\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}}})
, the ideal solution has
\ell_{1}
norm at most
(1+\epsilon)\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}
. Thus,
\|\mathbf{r}^{(t_{0},t]}\|_{1}\leq(1+(1+O(\frac{1}{t}))\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{10})\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}\leq(1+\frac{\epsilon}{5})\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}
.
As for
\mathbf{r}^{[0,t_{0}]}
, from
Lemma .16
, we have
\|\mathbf{r}^{[0,t_{0}]}\|_{1}\leq\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda_{\min}}}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}
.
Combine the two, we have

\left|\|\mathbf{r}(t)\|_{1}-\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}}{5}+\frac{t_{0}\cdot\left(\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda_{\min}}}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\right)}{t}\leq\epsilon\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}},

where the last inequality holds since
t\geq\Omega(\frac{m^{2}\cdot n\cdot\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}\cdot\lambda_{\min}\cdot\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}})
. This completes the proof for
Theorem 0.4
.
∎

Proofs for the properties of ideal and auxiliary SNN

Proof of
Lemma .8
.

Let us start with an observation on
Definition .12
about the points on the boundary of the ideal polytope
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
.

Claim .6
.

If
F
is non-degenerate, then for any
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}\in\partial\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
,
\text{rank}(F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})})=|\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})|
. Thus,
F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})}^{\top}F_{\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})}
is positive definite.

Next, let us show that for
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}\neq\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
,
S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}}\cap S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}}=\emptyset
. It is trivially true when at least one of them does not lie on the boundary
25
25

25

Note that
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}
does not lie on the boundary of
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
if and only if
\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}})=\emptyset
.
of
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
. Now, consider the case where both of them lie on the boundary of
\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}
and denote their active set as
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\textit{ideal}}_{1})
and
\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{\textit{ideal}}_{2})
. To prove from contradiction, suppose there exists
\mathbf{u}\in S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}}\cap S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}}
. By definition, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}+F_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}+F_{\Gamma_{2}}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{2},

where
\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{z}_{2}\geq 0
. Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}
. Consider the following cases.

•

(
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}
) By
Definition .12
, we have
F_{\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}=F_{\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}=\mathbf{1}
and thus

(\mathbf{z}_{2}-\mathbf{z}_{1})^{\top}F_{\Gamma}^{\top}F_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{z}_{2}-\mathbf{z}_{1})=(\mathbf{z}_{2}-\mathbf{z}_{1})^{\top}F_{\Gamma}^{\top}(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2})=0.

As
F_{\Gamma}^{\top}F_{\Gamma}
is positive definite by Claim
.6
, we have
\mathbf{z}_{1}=\mathbf{z}_{2}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}=\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}
, which is a contradiction.

•

(
\Gamma_{1}\neq\Gamma_{2}
) Without loss of generality, assume
\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma\neq\emptyset
and
\mathbf{z}_{1}\neq\mathbf{0}
. By
Definition .12
, we have

\displaystyle F_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma_{2}}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle>\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle F_{\Gamma_{2}\backslash\Gamma_{1}}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle F_{\Gamma}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0}.

As
\mathbf{z}_{1}\neq\mathbf{0}
, we then have

\displaystyle\|F_{\Gamma_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{2}-F_{\Gamma_{1}}\mathbf{z}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(F_{\Gamma_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{2}-F_{\Gamma_{1}}\mathbf{z}_{1}\right)^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-F_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma}\mathbf{z}_{1}|_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma}\right)^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(F_{\Gamma_{2}\backslash\Gamma}\mathbf{z}_{2}|_{\Gamma_{2}\backslash\Gamma}\right)^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(F_{\Gamma}\mathbf{z}_{2}|_{\Gamma}-F_{\Gamma}\mathbf{z}_{1}|_{\Gamma}\right)^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle<0.

Note that the reason why the last inequality holds is because
\left(-F_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma}\mathbf{z}_{1}|_{\Gamma_{1}\backslash\Gamma}\right)^{\top}\left(\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}_{2}\right)<0
.

Finally, it is easy to see that
\{S_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}}\}_{\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}\in\mathcal{P}_{F,1-\tau}}
covers
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
and thus we conclude that it is indeed a partition for
\mathcal{P}_{F,1}
.
∎

Proofs for the convergent analysis of solving
\ell_{1}
minimization

Proof of
Lemma .16
.

For any
t\geq 0
, define the following perturbed program of (
) and its dual.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{r}}{\text{minimize}}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}

(121)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=0

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t))^{\top}\mathbf{u}

(122)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\|F\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}\leq 1.

Note that
\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
is treated as a given constant to the optimization program. It turns out that the ideal algorithm optimizes this primal-dual perturbed program at time
t
with the following parameters.

Lemma .17
.

For any
t\geq 0
,
(\mathbf{r}^{*},\mathbf{u}^{*})=(\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t),\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
is the optimal solutions of (
121
).

Proof of
Lemma .17
.

We simply check the KKT condition. Since the program can be rewritten as a linear program, it satisfies the regularity condition of the KKT condition.

First, the primal and the dual feasibility can be verified by the dynamics of ideal algorithm. That is,
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{*}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=0
and
\|F\mathbf{u}^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Next, consider the Lagrangian of
Equation 121
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}-F\mathbf{u}.

Now, let’s verify that the gradient of the Lagrangian over
\mathbf{x}
is vanishing at
(\mathbf{r}^{*},\mathbf{u}^{*})=(\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t),\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
. That is,
0\in\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{r}^{*},\mathbf{u}^{*})=\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}-F\mathbf{u}
. Consider two cases as follows. For any
i\in[n]
,

(1)

When
i,-i\notin\Gamma^{\text{ideal}}(t)
. We have
\Big(\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\Big)_{i}=0
, i.e., the sub-gradient of the
i
th coordinate of
\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}
lies in
[-1,1]
. As
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\in[-1,1]
, we have
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\in\partial_{\mathbf{r}_{i}}\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}
.

(2)

When
i\in\Gamma^{\text{ideal}}(t)
(or
-i\in\Gamma^{\text{ideal}}(t)
). We have
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=1
(or
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=-1
). As
\text{sgn}\Big(\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\Big)_{i}=1
(or
\text{sgn}\Big(\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\Big)_{i}=-1
), we have
F_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=\text{sgn}\Big(\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\Big)_{i}=\partial_{\mathbf{r}_{i}}\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}
.

Finally, the complementary slackness is satisfied because
F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{*}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)=0
.
As a result, we conclude that
(\mathbf{r}^{*},\mathbf{u}^{*})=(\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t),\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t))
is the optimal solution of (
121
).
∎

Next, we are going to use the perturbation lemma in the Chapter 5.6 of
boyd2004convex
stated as follows.

Lemma .18

(perturbation lemma)
.

Given the following two optimization programs.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{r}}{\text{minimize}}

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{r})

(123)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle h(\mathbf{r})=\mathbf{0}.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{r}}{\text{minimize}}

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{r})

(124)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle h(\mathbf{r})=\mathbf{y}.

Let
\textsf{OPT}^{\text{original}}
be the optimal value of the original program
Equation 123
and
\textsf{OPT}^{\text{perturbed}}
be the optimal value of the perturbed program
Equation 124
. Let
\mathbf{u}^{*}
be the optimal dual value of the perturbed program
Equation 124
. We have

\textsf{OPT}^{\text{original}}\geq\textsf{OPT}^{\text{perturbed}}+\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{*}.

(125)

Now, think of
Equation 33
as the original program and
Equation 121
as the perturbed program. Namely,
f(\mathbf{r})=\|\mathbf{r}\|_{1}
,
h(\mathbf{r})=F^{\top}\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{x}
, and
\mathbf{y}=F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)-\mathbf{x}
. By the perturbation lemma, we have

\displaystyle\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}+\Big(F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)-\mathbf{x}\Big)^{\top}\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t).

As a result, the following upper bound holds.

\|\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{1}\leq\textsf{OPT}^{\ell_{1}}+\|\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}-F^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}.

(126)

Finally, as
\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)
lies in the feasible region
\{\mathbf{u}:\ F\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}
and the range space of
F
, we can upper bound the
\|\mathbf{u}^{\text{ideal}}(t)\|_{2}
term in
Equation 126
as follows.

Lemma .19
.

For every
\mathbf{u}
in the range space of
F
and
\|F\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we have
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda_{\min}}}
.

Proof of
Lemma .19
.

As
\mathbf{u}
lies in the range space of
F
, we have
\|F\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\geq\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}
. Also, because
\|F\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we have
\|F\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}
. As a result,

\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\|F\mathbf{u}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda_{\min}}}.

∎

By
Equation 126
and
Lemma .19
,
Lemma .16
holds. ∎
